Say "compile + logic proven, render not" and hand the click to the user.
- **One `cookies()` read in a SHARED SHELL component silently de-caches the ENTIRE site — and the build's route table will not tell you.** `AppHeader` is on every page. It resolved the session and read the `qb_exam` cookie during server render, so Next marked every route dynamic: **there were literally ZERO prerendered HTML files on disk**, and the `revalidate` settings on 77 `/notes` chapters, 10 `/guide` subtrees, the homepage, the `/mock` catalogue and the `/questions` landing pages had **never once taken effect** — for months. Every page served `Cache-Control: private, no-cache, no-store`. **The route table is NOT the check:** a route with `generateStaticParams` still prints `●` (SSG) even when every prerender bailed, so the build output looked correct throughout. **The only reliable probe is the filesystem** — `find .next/server/app -name '*.html' | wc -l`. If that number is 0, nothing is cached, whatever the legend says. Corollaries: (a) audit the shared shell (`layout.tsx`, `AppHeader`, any always-rendered component) for `cookies()`/`headers()`/`auth.getUser()` **before** optimising any individual page — a page-level fix is worthless while the shell de-caches everything; (b) `createSupabaseServerClient()` reads cookies internally, so calling it counts; (c) once fixed, prove cache SAFETY from the artifacts, not from reasoning — grep every `.html` **and `.rsc`** for emails / org names / authed-only controls, since a cached page is one copy served to everyone (2026-07-29: 622 pages, 0 leaks). See [[shell-component-decaches-site]].
- **Making a page cacheable BREAKS anything that relied on a server re-render, and the failures are silent.** Two fired on the same day: (1) `ExamPill` wrote the exam cookie then called `router.refresh()` so the SERVER would recompute the nav hrefs — on a cached page a refresh just re-serves the same copy, so the switcher would have appeared to do nothing (fixed by moving the active slug into client state). (2) `useSearchParams()` anywhere in the tree **bails a static prerender out to client rendering** — `QuestionList`'s bookmark button did this, silently costing the landing pages the caching they exist for; the fix is a `<Suspense>` boundary, and the symptom is only visible as a missing `.html` file. Grep for `router.refresh()` and `useSearchParams()` before making a route static. See [[next-caching-traps]].
- **Source-source subtopic name collisions during bulk chapter merges.** When canonicalizing N source chapters into one target chapter, a bulk `UPDATE subtopics SET chapter_id = '<tgt>' WHERE chapter_id IN ('<src1>', '<src2>', ...)` can fail with `23505` on `subtopics_chapter_id_name_key` even if a pre-flight source-vs-target collision check came back clean — because two sources may share a same-named subtopic that collides only once both are reparented. Bit us on the NDA Physics E&M cluster (5 sources → "Magnetic Field Inside a Solenoid" appeared in two of them). **Mitigation:** process sources sequentially via a `DO $$ FOR mapping IN (VALUES (...)) LOOP ... END $$` block; each iteration redirects colliding questions, deletes colliding source subtopics, reparents the rest, updates remaining question chapter_ids, and deletes the source chapter — so by the time source 2 merges, source 1's content is already in target and the standard collision-handling kicks in. Pattern + full template in `[[reclassification-sql-pattern]]`.
- **Catch-all chapters and subtopics drift until you reclassify per-question.** Buckets named "Mechanics", "Physics", "Error Identification in Sentences" collect every question that doesn't cleanly fit a more-specific name. They look harmless during a chapter-level merge but rot the bank's usefulness for technique drills. **Mitigation:** when a chapter or subtopic holds ≤~50 mixed-topic questions, read each stem and produce a `(question_id, target_canonical_id)` mapping; execute as a `DO $$ FOR m IN (VALUES (qid, tgt)) LOOP ... END $$` that (a) looks up the question's source subtopic, (b) finds same-named subtopic in target chapter, (c) redirects+deletes on collision, otherwise reparents+moves chapter_id. Precedents: Spotting Errors 29 q (2026-05-15 NDA English); Mechanics 19 q + Physics 26 q catch-all chapters (2026-05-15 NDA Physics). Beyond ~50 q the user prefers chapter-level merge + subtopic-cleanup as a follow-up pass.
- **`/notes` subtopic slugs are GLOBALLY unique, not chapter-scoped.** `question_concept_tags` keys on `(question_id, subtopic_slug, concept_slug)` with **no chapter or subject column**, and `loadConceptDrills` / the mastery-checkpoint query filter by `subtopic_slug` ALONE. So two chapters that both use a slug like `foundations` will cross-contaminate each other's drill lists + checkpoints. Bit us 2026-05-30: MHT-CET Indefinite Integration reused Sound's `foundations` slug — notes-lint caught it as "DB tag has unknown concept_slug" because the other chapter's concepts don't match. **Mitigation:** every notes subtopic slug must be unique across ALL chapters in `NOTES_CHAPTERS` (the URL segment can repeat per subject route, but the slug used as the concept-tag key cannot). MHT-CET's foundation subtopic was renamed `foundations` → `fundamentals`. notes-lint surfaces the collision but only *after* tags are inserted — pick distinctive slugs up front.
- **Renaming a SHIPPED chapter silently rots its `/guide` + `/notes` cross-refs.** The DB taxonomy and the guide/notes editorial TS are joined only by NAME at request time (`resolveTaxonomy`), so a Phase-D rename/merge leaves the TS pointing at a dead subtopic — the "Drill the N →" CTA resolves to an EMPTY `/browse` filter, no error. Bit us twice on 2026-05-30: the Sound notes 5→4 redesign broke the `/guide/nda-physics` Sound playbook + strategy `mustDrill` + CHAPTER_TABLE prose; the 2026-05-29 Probability merge ("Probability with Dice" → "Probability via Counting") broke two `nda-maths` principle drills + the Probability tier. **Mitigation:** after any rename/merge of a shipped chapter, sync `/guide` `_data` (playbooks `subtopics[]`, strategy `mustDrill`/`skipSubtopics`, principles `drill`, compounds filters, prose counts) + `/notes` `_data` + concept tags, then run `npm test` — the 7 playbook guides + CA themes + Maths each have a `describe.skipIf(!HAS_ENV)` live-taxonomy resolution test (`guide-nda-<subject>-playbooks.test.ts`; `guide-nda-maths-taxonomy.test.ts` for Template A) that fails on drill-target drift. Full procedure: [[shipped-chapter-rename-downstream-sync]]. The `/browse` backlink chip (`getQuestionResources` name lookup) + the single-page Economics chapter CTA are the cross-link surfaces still untested.
- **Integration-test fixtures leak into the LIVE bank when `afterAll` is interrupted — SOLVED STRUCTURALLY 2026-08-06: `npm test` now targets a DEDICATED TEST Supabase project, never prod.** The old world: every DB-integration test wrote throwaway rows into the single prod project, cleaned by `afterAll` + an org-keyed teardown sweep. The 2026-08-06 incident proved that net had **three structural blind spots** — org-less platform tables (`mock_tests`, `quizzes`), fixtures written INTO the real org by the `/api/sync/mock` route, and `@test.invalid` emails vs the `@test.local` sweep — and the "one universal signal is `org_id`" claim false. Leaked fixtures were publicly visible on `/mock`, `/browse` and `/quiz`; two real students sat a 1-question fake mock. **The fix:** test project `rjwuwmrzkyergflmmfxq` (separate free Supabase account → own quotas); `tests/setup.ts` + `tests/helpers/testdb.ts` resolve `.env.test.local`/`TEST_SUPABASE_*` and **hard-refuse** to run fixture-writing tests against any project ref not on the allow-list (fails closed on unknown refs). Read-only **prod-contract** suites (editorial ↔ live-content checks, listed in `tests/prodContractFiles.ts`) still run against prod via `npm run test:prod-contract`. Runbook + gotchas (idle pause, seed shape, missing-migration detection): `scripts/testdb/README.md`; `npm run testdb:reset` re-baselines the test DB. The replay also exposed that **a prod-applied migration whose `.sql` was never committed (0021, recreated) makes prod unrebuildable — `testdb:migrate` is now the standing check for that class.**

## Design axes

Load-bearing design distinctions for the project. Each one earns its place by being structural — getting the axis wrong has cost real work, and the answer to "which axis does this live on?" is non-obvious for the named feature areas.

### Tag taxonomies — principles (horizontal) vs concepts (vertical)

**`/guide` principles** and **`/notes` concepts** are different kinds of tag and must NEVER share a schema or be merged into one taxonomy.

| | Principles | Concepts |
|---|---|---|
| Axis | Horizontal — cross-subtopic | Vertical — within-subtopic |
| Cardinality | ~20 in NDA Maths | ~5–10 per subtopic × hundreds = thousands at scale |
| Storage | TS modules (`/guide/.../principles.ts`) + curated `extraQuestionIds` | DB table `question_concept_tags` (migration 0021) |
| Authoring | Editorial — hand-curated, slow update cadence | Per-upload — admin tagging UI or chat session, fast cadence |
| Value comes from | **Selectivity** (only the top-20, hand-picked) | **Coverage** (every relevant question tagged) |
| Identity question | "What unifying lens does this question demand?" | "What specific technique within this chapter?" |

**The wall — non-negotiable:**
- DON'T migrate `/guide` principles to a DB table. Hand-curation is the signal-to-noise mechanism; "scaling" them dilutes their value.
- DON'T bundle principles into `question_concept_tags` via a `tag_kind` discriminator. Flattens the strategic distinction.
- DO share infrastructure at the seam: one admin tagging UI on `/dashboard/questions/[id]/edit`, one notes-lint validator, future LLM-suggest can suggest both kinds. Sharing UI ≠ sharing schema.

**Why this earned a section:** The initial concept-tags proposal (Phase 1, 2026-05-17) was a unified `question_tags` table. User pushed back; I conceded. Both axes shipped separately (principles still in TS, concepts in migration 0021) and the wall has held. This section exists so future tag-related features (admin UI, LLM-suggest, AI tutor grounding, MHT_CET_AI integration) don't re-litigate it from scratch.

### Cross-link backlinks — chapter-level (automatic name lookup) vs tag-level (manual DB-tagged override)

The `/browse` QuestionCard chip row (Tier 1 + Tier 1.5, 2026-05-18) renders **two distinct classes of backlink** and the wall between them matters. Same dropdown of chips, two completely different authoring loops and data sources.

| | Chapter-level chips | Tag-level chips |
|---|---|---|
| Examples | "Playbook: Sound" · "Concept notes" | "Lever: Vieta" · "Concept: Weighted Mean" |
| Source | Name-based lookup at render time | DB row in `question_principle_tags` / `question_concept_tags` |
| Authoring cost | **Zero per question** — purely a function of `(exam, subject, chapter, subtopic)` | **Manual per question** — chat tagging session, INSERT with `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` |
| Coverage | 100% of questions whose chapter has a guide / subtopic has notes | Only questions explicitly tagged (today: 488 principle tags · 262 concept tags) |
| Failure mode | Silent break when DB chapter renamed (no lint catch yet — see ROADMAP "notes-lint guide-side rename validation") | Silent fallback to generic chip when slug doesn't resolve via `getPrincipleName` / `getConceptName` |
| Override behaviour | The default; renders for all qualifying questions | When present, **replaces** the chapter-level chip for the same axis (more specific wins) |
| Resolver | `getQuestionResources({exam, subject, chapter, subtopic})` in `src/lib/links/questionResources.ts` | `getQuestionResources({...}, tags)` — same function, second arg from `getResourceTagsForQuestions` batch |

**The wall — design intent:**
- DON'T expand the chapter-level lookup to do "fuzzy" matching when a chapter rename happens. The failure mode (chip disappears) is the correct signal — fix the rename, don't paper over the drift.
- DON'T attempt to back-fill tag-level chips from the chapter-level data (e.g. auto-create a `Lever: <chapter-name>` chip when no principle tag exists). That destroys the curated-vs-automatic distinction and floods low-leverage questions with meaningless tag chips.
- DO let tag-level chips quietly fall through to chapter-level chips when the tag's slug doesn't resolve. Better a generic "NDA Maths strategy" chip than a broken `/principles/<unknown-slug>` link.
- DO share infrastructure at the seam: both axes flow through the same `getQuestionResources(input, tags?)` function, both render via the same `<ResourceChip>` component in QuestionCard. The two-tier system is a function-signature distinction (optional second arg) not two separate code paths.

**Why this earned a section:** The 2026-05-18 Tier 1.5 ship added tag-level overrides AFTER the Tier 1 chapter-level chips were in production. The temptation in the moment was to refactor into "one unified backlink table" or to have tag-level chips deduplicate against chapter-level chips. Both ideas are wrong — they conflate two different authoring loops (curated tagging session vs no-touch render-time mapping) and two different coverage shapes (long-tail per-question vs blanket-per-chapter). The split shipped cleanly because the function signature preserved the asymmetry: `tags?` is optional, the resolver short-circuits chapter-level chips when a more-specific tag-chip exists, and untagged questions still get the generic chip — graceful fallback throughout. Sister wall to the tag-taxonomies axis above.

### Guide strand axis — execution-mode (chapter-partitioning) vs tier-style (chapter-tiering)

Within Template B variants, the **strand split** is the strategic axis the strategy page renders against. There are two fundamentally different ways to construct it, and they are NOT interchangeable — picking the wrong one produces a strategy axis that sorts questions by nothing meaningful.

| | Execution-mode strands | Tier-style strands |
|---|---|---|
| Subjects | NDA English (R/Rule/Reason), NDA Chemistry (R/Rule/Calculate), NDA Biology (R/Apply/Verify), NDA Geography (R/Apply/Verify) | NDA History (Cornerstone/Foundation Recall/Quick-Win) |
| Strand semantics | Each strand is a SKILL bucket (recall a fact / apply a rule / trace a mechanism / evaluate a statement). Chapters get grouped into a strand by their dominant execution mode. | Each strand is a STRATEGIC TIER (cornerstone weight / foundation recall / quick-win). Chapters get grouped into a strand by their bank share + %HARD profile. |
| Recall slot | Stable — there's always a Recall strand (named-fact recall is universal in Indian entrance exams). | Stable in spirit (Foundation Recall) but the slot SEMANTICS bent — it now groups chapters by tier, not by execution mode. |
| When to pick | Execution modes PARTITION chapters: one cluster of chapters is 60%+ dominated by one mode, others are 20%- on that mode. | Execution modes are UNIFORM across all chapters (25–45% everywhere) — can't partition. AND/OR one chapter has dominant bank share (40%+) that warrants its own Cornerstone tier. |
| Decision probe | For each candidate mode (recall/rule/apply/verify/calculate/reason/multi-statement/date-anchored), count `q × chapter_spread` AND per-chapter %. Top mode at 60%+ in one cluster = execution-mode strands work. | Top mode is 25–45% of every chapter = uniform = falls back to tier-style. Use bank-share + %HARD per chapter as the partition signal instead. |
| HARD calibration | Strand-level (densest-HARD strand called out — Geography's Apply at 23% avg HARD). | Implicit in the tier-structure — Cornerstone IS the densest-HARD tier by construction (History's Cornerstone = Modern India 34% HARD). |

**The wall — design intent:**
- DON'T force execution-mode strands when modes don't partition. History's multi-statement (70 q × 4 ch · 43% HARD) and date-anchored (61 q × 4 ch · 43% HARD) cut UNIFORMLY across chapters — a Bio/Geo-style R/Apply/Verify split would force lopsided cuts (Recall 44 q : Date 163 q : Verify 53 q if grouped by chapter's dominant mode). The strategy axis would sort by nothing meaningful.
- DON'T force tier-style strands when execution modes DO partition cleanly. English's Vocab/Idioms (Recall) and RC/Cloze (Reason) chapters are genuinely different SKILL types, not just different priorities — a Cornerstone/Foundation/Quick-Win split would lose that information.
- DO let the decision probe pick. Test execution-mode first (Template B default); fall back to tier-style only when modes are uniform.
- DO share infrastructure at the seam: both axes use the same `STRATEGY_STRANDS` data shape (each strand has `id`, `label`, `pitch`, `approach`, `chapters: StrandChapter[]`) and the same strategy-page renderer. The two-axis distinction is in the BUCKET MEANINGS (`PlaybookBucket = "recall" | "apply" | "verify"` for Bio/Geo vs `"cornerstone" | "foundation" | "quickwin"` for History) — same code path, different semantics.

**Why this earned a section:** The 2026-05-19 NDA History ship was the first time Template B's strand axis itself bent. Before History, every Template B variant had bent the second + third SLOTS within an execution-mode axis (Rule→Apply for Bio, Calculate→Verify for Chem) — but the axis itself was always execution-mode. History exposed the next level: when execution modes don't partition chapters at all, the axis itself has to change. The temptation in the moment was to force one of the existing slot-bend patterns onto History (most naturally R/Apply/Verify like Bio + Geo) — that would have shipped a strategy page where students see "Apply chapters" and "Verify chapters" labels but no chapter actually leans either way, sorting by nothing. The tier-style fallback shipped cleanly because the strand data shape was already general (chapters grouped under labelled strands with a pitch + approach) — only the bucket vocabulary changed. Sister wall to the tag-taxonomies + cross-link-backlinks axes above: in all three, the right call is to preserve an asymmetric distinction at the shared seam rather than to flatten for elegance.


### Content-organisation axis — book-structure (`section_*`, /board) vs conceptual (`subtopic`, /browse + /notes)

A board-textbook chapter is stored ONCE but organised on **two orthogonal axes**, and they must not be conflated:
- **Conceptual — `subtopic`** (`chapters`→`subtopics`, `order_index`): technique/topic grouping ("Combined Equation", "Angle Between Lines"). Drives `/browse` filters + `/notes` teaching order. A single book Exercise's questions are deliberately spread across several subtopics by concept.
- **Book-structure — `section_kind`/`section_group`/`section_label`/`section_seq`** (migration 0043): the book's physical layout (§2.1 Solved Examples → Exercise 2.1 → … → Miscellaneous), in reading order. Drives `/board` ONLY.

**The wall:** the `/board` reader groups by `section_group` (book section), NOT by `subtopic` — grouping by subtopic would shatter a single contiguous book Exercise into pieces under different headers. Conversely `/browse`+`/notes` never use `section_*`. The two axes are joined only by living on the same `questions` row. **Why it earned a spot:** the reader was first designed to group by `subtopic.order_index`; Matrices exposed that Exercise 2.2 (Q.1–6) splits across three subtopics, so the book axis had to be a separate, PDF-verified `section_*` outline (`scripts/stateboard/sections.ts`). Book order is NOT derivable from `subtopic` NOR reliably from the messy `question_number` string — it's authored per chapter and gated by `board:lint`. See [[board-reader]].

## Guide structure templates

The full template catalogue (A principles-first · B playbooks-first + variants · C chapter-playbooks+formula-sheet · single-page landing · D theme-prep-checklist), the per-subject variant tables, and the **"Choosing the template"** decision script live in **[GUIDE_TEMPLATES.md](GUIDE_TEMPLATES.md)** — **read it before proposing or building a new `/guide`**. Pick the template by analysing the bank first (bank-thinness gate → cross-chapter-lever survey → %HARD spread), never by copying precedent. Shared components (`GuideShell`/`GuideHero`/`StatBlock`/`BrowseLink`/`DriftTable`/`RelatedPlaybooks`/`ReferenceTables`) + the per-new-guide plumbing checklist (sitemap, Footer, /browse pill, parent cross-link, `questionResources.ts` mapping, `guide-strategy-headline` test) are documented there too.

## Engagement engine — principles gate (always-on)

The engagement engine ties PYQ Vault's scattered behaviour signals into a loop that improves learning + retention. **This gate is non-negotiable — like the "no slop" rule, every engagement mechanic must clear it before it ships.** Imported + adapted from the sibling English AI Tutor's hard-won engine (`Downloads/English_AI_Tutor`), whose findings we inherit rather than re-learn.

**Every mechanic must serve ≥1 learning principle** — else it's a dark pattern, not a feature:
- **Retrieval practice** — test recall, don't re-expose. **Spaced repetition** — resurface what the student got wrong until mastered (80%). **Immediate feedback** — results on the same/next screen, never a dashboard they must go find. **Deliberate practice** — drill the *specific* thing they missed, not "keep practising". **Mastery before progression** — gate on demonstrated 80%, don't reward accumulated low scores. **Metacognition** — every metric we track is shown to the student in plain language (trends, weak chapters, days-to-exam). **Growth-framed feedback** — name what went well + what's next, never a bare score.

**Forbidden (dark patterns — this is a stressed-teen exam-prep cohort):** leaderboards (demotivate the bottom half in a known-peer setting), hearts/lives (anxiety), variable rewards/loot boxes (exploitative), streak freezes/shields (complexity, no learning value), and **vanity milestones** ("logged in 3 days", "earned 100 XP" — awards must be genuine learning achievements).

**Two structural rules the AI Tutor learned the expensive way:**
1. **Measure the usage shape BEFORE building a mechanic.** They shipped 6 milestone kinds; 3 fired *zero* times because students study in **bursts, not daily** — daily streaks are silent demotivation for this cohort. For an exam app, **prefer deadline/goal-progress over daily streaks.** Read `user_activity` first; pick mechanics that fit the real data.
2. **Awards are server-side + idempotent** (dedupe_key), and **celebrations fire at the trigger surface** (the mock-result page), never a buried dashboard.

**The spine (built 2026-07-12):** `user_activity` (migration 0052) is the append-only event log every mechanic reads from — pure core `src/lib/activity/events.ts`, best-effort writer `src/lib/activity/service.ts` (never blocks the primary action), emitters on mock-submit / notes-mastery+checkpoint / bookmark, backfill `scripts/activity/backfill.ts`. **Signed-in only** for now (anon top-of-funnel stays SEO-clean). See [[project-engagement-engine]] + the Decisions entry below.

## Decisions log

Why behind architectural pivots — saves future-you from "why didn't we just…?".

**Convention:** topical intros (like "Foundations" below) sit at the top. Dated entries are bucketed by month — `### YYYY-MM` per month. When a new month begins, add a new `### YYYY-MM` sub-section above the prior one. **Within a month, entries are ordered newest-first (reverse chronological top-down): a new entry goes at the TOP of its month's block.** Net result: the most recent entry anywhere in the log is the first dated entry a reader sees after the foundations. This lets a reader collapse historical months mentally and focus on what's recent.

### Foundations (M1–M3, 2026-05-08)

The load-bearing choices made during the initial scaffold. Every later phase rests on these — re-litigate only with explicit reason.

- **No Prisma.** Supabase RLS is the security boundary; Prisma uses a single privileged DB user and bypasses RLS by default. Forcing per-request `SET LOCAL` JWT injection fights the tide. `@supabase/supabase-js` carries the user's JWT through naturally.
- **RLS helpers in `private` schema.** PostgREST exposes any `public.*` function as a `/rest/v1/rpc/<name>` endpoint. Supabase advisor flagged `current_user_org_id()` / `current_user_is_admin()` as callable by `anon` and `authenticated`. Functionally safe (returns `null`/`false` for anon) but defence-in-depth — moved to a schema PostgREST doesn't expose.
- **OMML via marker-and-post-process.** The `docx` library's `ImportedXmlComponent.fromXmlString` wraps imported XML in an `<undefined>` element that Word refuses to open. We render placeholder text where math should go, then patch `word/document.xml` after `Packer.toBuffer`. Sidesteps the library quirk; works for arbitrary OMML.
- **Word export = ZIP of two files.** First version was a single .docx with a 3-way layout toggle. User asked to split: a Question Paper distributable without leaking answers, and a separate Answer Key. One ZIP keeps the bundle obvious. **Superseded 2026-05-13** — ZIP killed for mobile UX; route now returns one .docx per request via a required `kind` selector.
- **Hard 200/export cap, no auto-split.** Vercel function timeout is the binding constraint. A clear error ("narrow filters") ships faster than a zip-of-zips. Revisit if a teacher genuinely needs 500 questions in one paper.
- **Chapters/subtopics auto-create, subjects don't.** Subjects are top-level taxonomy curated centrally; auto-creating "Phyiscs" from a typo would corrupt the canonical list. Chapters/subtopics are per-upload working data — small typo cost, and a merge tool can clean up later.

### 2026-08

- **2026-08-15 — Ch.9 Surface Area and Volume shipped: 34 q PUBLIC, mh-sb-9 Mathematics 840 → 874. Part 2 is 4 of 9. FOUR book defects — the most of any Part-2 chapter so far — and every one was found by re-deriving a printed answer rather than by any probe.**
  - **THE KEY IS COMPLETE AND PURELY NUMERIC, which is what made this chapter the easiest to audit and the most productive.** Every answer is an area, a volume or a length, so the cross-check is arithmetic rather than judgement — and **all 34 printed answers were re-derived**. Two agreed everywhere (PS 9.1 8/8, PS 9.3 7/7); the four failures are below.
  - **TWO STEM MISPRINTS, both diagnosed the same way: the printed answer is clean for exactly ONE nearby value.** **PS 9.2 Q5** gives a cone of volume 6280 cu.cm with base radius 30 cm and keys \(h = 15\); \(r = 30\) yields 6.67, while \(r = 20\) yields 15 EXACTLY. **PS 9.2 Q7** gives volume 1212 cu.cm with height 24 cm and keys 550 sq.cm — which needs \(r = 7\), and \(r = 7\) with \(h = 24\) has volume **1232**, not 1212. In both the key is right and the stem is wrong, the same shape as Ch.7's Q5.
  - **A UNIT MISPRINT: PS 9.3 Q1(iii)'s key reads "153.86 sq.m, 179.50 cubic cm"** — the two units disagree inside one answer. The radius is in centimetres, so it is 153.86 sq.CM; both VALUES are correct. Caught by reading the key as a whole rather than one entry at a time.

A tank has glass at BOTH ends of each horizontal dimension, so 0.4 cm comes off each: \(60.4 - 0.4 = 60\), \(40.4 - 0.4 = 40\), and the height loses 0.2 for the base alone, giving \(60 	imes 40 	imes 40 = 96000\). **All three inner measurements coming out whole is the tell** — the outer dimensions were chosen to make them so.

Both independent routes agree (x + y = 180 via AB || CD, and x = z as corresponding angles via AB || EF), which is what makes the diagnosis safe rather than a guess. The corruption is arithmetic and silent: **exponents are separate 7.6 pt spans against a 13–14 pt body**, so `get_text()` flattens \(63x^2 + 5x - 2\) to `63x2 + 5x - 2` — *a different polynomial* — 575 times in Ch.3 alone; and **stacked fractions interleave into unrecoverable word order**, \(\frac{a-2b}{a+2b}\) arriving as `2 2 a b a b - +`, 256 times in Ch.4. Two more traps measured: the rupee sign is a font hack (`RupeeForadian` mapped onto the backtick, so every amount would reach the DB as `` ` 1200 ``), and Ch.5 is the one chapter where the text layer nearly works (9 sub-body spans, 15 fraction bars) — **still read as images, so the volume has ONE contract and a later session need not re-derive which chapters are exceptions.**

Each of the five Problem sets also words its MCQ instruction differently ("Write the correct alternative" / "Select the appropriate alternative" / "Choose the correct alternative answers"), so a transcription contract must key on the block's SHAPE, never one phrase.

Two are stem misprints where **the book's own printed solution refutes its own stem**: `5.2 SolvedEx.3` says "9 less than 4 times the original number" while its working uses \(2(10y+x) - 9\) and its answer 36 is consistent only with that (as printed the digits come out as \(y = \tfrac13\)); **Ex 5.2 Q1**'s "5 rupee notes are less by 10" gives \(15y = 400\), a non-whole number of notes, while the book's own answer needs the opposite direction. Two are incompleteness: **Ex 3.2 Q1(iii)** keys \(10n + m\) for a number whose tens digit is \(m\), and **Ex 3.1 Q9(iv)** omits \(3x^2 + 5x\) from a list of binomials. (2) **`apply-errata.ts` mirrored the bracket to the DB but not to the source** for any chapter whose fragments are not named `.s<digit>` / `.misc` — my `b1-ps51` bands matched nothing, so the bracket lived only in the database and the next re-commit would have reverted it. It now searches all non-scratch fragments, **warns loudly when a bracket mirrors nowhere**, and — the part that matters — performs the source mirror **independently of the DB skip**, so re-running heals a previous half-application. That self-heal is what recovered the four Ch.5 brackets. (3) Fixing (2) introduced a regression I caught by reading the output: fragments sorted before `ex.solutions.json`, so three exercise rows got a bracket-only `solution` written into their *transcription* source, which `commit.ts` would read as an answered row. - **2026-08-14 — The Chemistry syllabus map was reporting a bank that no longer existed: JEE Chemistry 149 subtopics / 731 PYQ against a live 202 / 3,455. They were, by eight days.
  - **THE CAUSE IS ORDERING, AND IT IS THE WHOLE LESSON: a bank spine is a SNAPSHOT with the PYQ count baked into each row's name, and nothing re-derives it.** JEE Chemistry completed on 2026-08-06 (259 → 3,455 q) *after* its spine was built. Physics and Maths were measured and are EXACT on all three exams — because their spines were ingested *after* their corpora completed. Same script, same data model; only the order differed. MHT-CET and NDA Chemistry were also exact (122/2,165 and 47/261), so the drift was one exam's.
  - **53 subtopics worth 318 PYQ existed in the bank and had NO ROW ON THE PAGE AT ALL**, and 145 of the 149 rows that did exist carried a stale PYQ count — the number that ORDERS the gap list a teacher prioritises from. `Stoichiometry and Concentration` (38 PYQ), `Nucleophilic Addition` (28) and `Van't Hoff Factor` (16) were among the invisible.
  - **THE WORST DEFECT WAS A RENDERER, NOT DATA — and it is [[default-becomes-assertion]] one layer out.** `MappingTable` branched only on `status === "not"`, so a row with NO RULING fell into the same arm as a row ruled covered-but-scattered and printed **"no single section"** — an affirmative claim that the book teaches the topic diffusely. Measured: **53 unassessed against 2 genuinely diffuse, so 96% of the phrase was false**, across 106 cells. A reader saw *Stoichiometry and Concentration — 38 PYQ — "no single section"* in the State Board column when Std XI Ch.1 IS that chapter and nobody had looked. Fixed by a pure `coverCellState` (TDD) separating four states, with unassessed rendered unstyled — no colour chip, because it is the absence of a finding. The coverage summary gained a **Not assessed** column for the same reason: without it `full + partial + not` silently failed to reach `live` and read as an arithmetic error. Scope was checked, not assumed: all three NCERT tables are fully ruled at top level and the Physics/Maths exam spines are 100% ruled, so all 106 false cells were on this one page.
  - **A SECOND, PRE-EXISTING DEFECT FOUND WHILE VERIFYING: the alignment table rendered 410 rows on one request and 438 on the next, off identical data, with authored pairings swinging 188 vs 90.** `fetchAll` paged with `.range()` and **no `ORDER BY`**; LIMIT/OFFSET without one has no stability guarantee, so consecutive pages could repeat and skip rows. This had been hitting every request, independent of the refresh. Pinned to each table's unique key (`id`; `(concept_id, exam)`, verified unique across all 9,100 rows) — three runs now byte-identical, Physics and Maths too. **The tell was two "identical" runs disagreeing; a single run looked fine.**
  - **RE-INGESTING A SPINE IS A MIGRATION, NOT A RE-RUN, because `section_no` is positional ACROSS ALL THREE EXAMS.** Adding 53 JEE rows moved MHT-CET from MHT-150.. to MHT-203.. and NDA from NDA-272.. to NDA-325..; surviving refs were upserted IN PLACE (ruling still attached, now describing a different subtopic) and the rest were pruned, cascading their rulings away (`concept_id` is `ON DELETE CASCADE`). **636 authored links were in the blast radius**; the apply pruned exactly 100 rows, as predicted. New `renumber-rulings.ts` + pure `renumberRulings` (7 TDD cases) maps old ref → new through **(chapter, subtopic)** — the subtopic name alone is NOT unique, six Chemistry names live in two chapters each — and refuses wholesale rather than renumbering the rows it understood. Verified two ways: the ruling files' diff touched **only `section_no` lines, 314+/314−, zero other lines**, and `commit-bank-rulings`' name guard passed on all three files. Procedure now documented in `ingest-bank-spine.ts` itself: snapshot → apply → renumber + re-commit, backup first.
  - **`liveFromYear` was investigated at length and deliberately left at 2023.** The instinct to raise it to 2026 (correct for JEE, 7 dead chapters) is wrong because **the constant is shared across three exams whose corpora end in different years**: MHT-CET Chemistry has NO 2026 papers, so 2026 marks all 30 of its chapters dead and collapses its row to `live = 0`; NDA loses 5, three of them merely unsampled in a single 15-question 2026 sitting. At 2025 NDA still loses 2, on a corpus averaging ~29 questions a year over 12 chapters where absence is noise. Measured cost of leaving it: **3 rows of history on a 37-row gap list.** The comment was rewritten to say the constant is a **JEE-only fact** and that the per-exam dead-set keying is a *containment barrier*, not a feature. Same bug shape as the 2026-08-04 fix, one level up — that fix made the dead SET per-exam and left the THRESHOLD shared.
  - **All 53 adjudicated from the corpus: 69 full · 17 partial · 20 not, every verdict citing a read section.** The dominant finding is NCERT's rationalisation — no States of Matter, Solid State, Surface Chemistry, Polymers, p-Block or Environmental chapters — so 15 of the 20 `not` verdicts are NCERT gaps the State Board covers in full: `unit cell` and `packing efficiency` are **ZERO across the entire NCERT corpus** against 133 and 17 State Board hits, `Boyle` 29 vs zero, `catalysis`/`surface tension`/`silicone`/`nanomaterial` likewise zero. It runs the other way twice: **corrosion** appears exactly twice in the whole State Board corpus, both adjectival asides, and **soil pollution** is absent from both books.
  - **THREE RULINGS WOULD HAVE BEEN WRONG ON HIT COUNTS ALONE**, which is the ruling brief's central warning proved again. NCERT's only `Hofmann` hit is **Hofmann's *bromamide* reaction in a list of named reactions** — an amine preparation, not the elimination the subtopic asks about (`not`, where the count implied coverage). `Moseley` is one qualitative sentence in each book with no relation and no K/L series (`partial`, not `full`). And **the State Board spells titrimetric as "titrametric"**, so every spelling searched returned zero until the paragraph behind a `volumetric` hit was read. All three were settled by a new `corpus_context.py`, which prints the sentences around a term — the tooling could count hits but could not show text, while the brief's rule is that only the chapter that TEACHES the topic counts.
  - **A TOOL THAT WORDED AN OPERATOR ERROR AS A FINDING, and it nearly shipped ~12 false verdicts.** `search_corpus.py --source` matched exactly, so `--source="MH State"` (the obvious guess for "MH State Board") selected zero chapters and every term printed *"ZERO hits in MH State — absence is weak evidence"*. Absence is what that tool is most often used to establish, so a typo silently manufactured it across every term at once — producing exactly the confident, plausible, unfalsifiable `not` rulings the brief says no probe can catch. Caught only because "physical properties" returning zero in a chemistry textbook is implausible. It now lists the corpus's real source names and exits 2. **Same family as the "no single section" bug: a surface converting *we did not look* into *we looked and found nothing*.**
  - **A NEW STANDING PROBE, because the reason this sat wrong for eight days is that nothing checked it.** Every other invariant here has one — `audit-directions`, `audit-alignment`, `audit-spine`, `board:lint`, `coverage.ts` — and spine freshness had none; it was found by a human doubting a number. `audit-spine-freshness` diffs each spine against the live bank per (chapter, subtopic) and is wired into `syllabus:audit`. **Proven to fire rather than merely tested**: diffed against the pre-refresh snapshot it reports exactly the drift that existed that morning (JEE 53 missing / 318 PYQ, MHT-CET and NDA clean), matching the independent SQL. TRIAGE not gate — drift is the expected state between an ingest and the next refresh, and failing a build for a condition whose remedy is a careful migration trains people to skip the chain.
  - **`audit-directions` was suppressing retired content with a HARDCODED LIST OF CHAPTER NAMES, wrong in both directions.** It had rotted (it recorded Chemistry's chapters as dead "after 2021"; the completed corpus shows 2023) and it was at the wrong GRAIN: JEE retired content at SUBTOPIC level and our taxonomy files some of it into chapters still being set — `Gas Laws and Ideal Gas Equation` (13 PYQ, none since 2023) sits inside *Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry*, 205 PYQ and set in 2026. That produced **four contradictions where BOTH rulings were correct**: the State Board does teach gas laws, and JEE has stopped asking. Now derived from the bank per (chapter, subtopic), retiring anything whose newest PYQ predates 2024 — deliberately NOT the page's `liveFromYear`, since this probe audits only the JEE spine and can use the sharper rule. **Being derived, it stopped masking the inverse case and immediately surfaced 5 pre-existing contradictions the name list had hidden** — `Classification of Solids` (2024) and `Types of Polymers` (2025) are LIVE, against State Board rows asserting both chapters were dropped. Those 5 are real stale rulings, left flagged rather than edited: they are hand-authored rows in the 4,315-row block whose only source was the retired `ingest-exam-spine.ts`.
  - **A full wipe-and-rebuild was considered and REJECTED on an audit of what is regenerable.** Both book spines, the NCERT edge, the bank spines and all 318 bank-spine rulings rebuild from committed sources — but the **4,315 State Board → exam status rows have no source of record**, every one carrying its own authored note, and their only producer was deleted on 2026-08-04 for defaulting unruled pairs to `full`. A wipe had the same upside as the targeted refresh and put the one non-regenerable block at risk. The staleness was entirely inside the block designed to be regenerated.
  - Verified: gate PASS twice · 630 prerendered `.html` · unassessed **0** across all three exams · `202 live = 153 full + 32 partial + 17 not`. See [[syllabus-map]].

- **2026-08-14 — Cadetprep Worksheets Batch J COMPLETES THE SOURCE: the last 8 chapters with content = 1,255 q PUBLIC; the exam reaches 29 chapters / 7,376 q, the bank 46,254.** 3D Geometry 233 · Probability 222 · Statistics 189 · Properties of Triangle 183 · Definite Integration 140 · Conics 116 · Differential Equations 93 · Applications of Derivatives 79. Chapters 22 and 29 hold no worksheet files at all and are deliberately absent from the registry. 47 blind packets, then a second blind pass over every disputed row, then 8 twin-repair drafting runs: **28 key flips · 4 exclusions · ~180 option repairs.**
  - **THE HEADLINE INVERTS THE BATCH'S OWN PRIOR. `config.ts` flagged that six of these eight already sat at ~25% per letter and warned this could mean the keys were assigned to hit a distribution rather than derived — which would make them WORSE.** The measurement says neither: disputes ran **7.7% (97 of 1,259), below Batch I**, so the keys genuinely are better placed — but the option SETS were never checked for duplicates, producing **149 agreeing-twin rows, triple Batch I's repair load**. 3D Geometry alone runs **65 twins in 235 rows (28%)**. A better key distribution bought nothing for the student, who still faced the right answer printed twice.
  - **THE ILLUSORY-FLIP TRAP FIRED FOR THE THIRD BATCH RUNNING, and this time it was in my own tooling.** `crosstab.ts` matched twin claims in free prose, so a pass-2 note reading "p = 0 (equivalently q = 1)" landed in `FLIP?TWIN` — the one bucket a reviewer is primed to dismiss — **burying a genuine wrong key on a binomial mean-equals-variance row**. Tightened to the structured `TWIN: A=C` prefix the brief mandates. **A twin claim is a structured assertion, not a turn of phrase**; a filter over prose does not discriminate, it just moves the error somewhere quieter.
  - **THE MANDATORY `value` FIELD PAID FOR ITSELF TWICE.** Two Definite Integration rows the crosstab called CONFLICT (03-16, 03-17) were actually twins: both passes computed the *identical value* and named different letters of an equal pair. Comparing two letters would have sent both to hand-derivation as disagreements; comparing two derivations of the same QUANTITY exposed them immediately.
  - **THREE KEY-STANDS — rows where BOTH blind passes were wrong and the source key was right.** The sharpest is Statistics 05-5: given a non-zero sum of deviations, the precise conclusion is *the value used is not the mean* (keyed), not the looser "error in calculation" both passes picked. Also Properties 07-16 (both passes read "using Napier's Analogy" as excluding the sine-rule route — a reading of the instruction, not a mathematical finding) and 3D 07-4 (proportional normals alone admit COINCIDENT planes, so the keyed condition is the stronger and correct one). This is the standing argument for never applying a letter disagreement automatically, and the first time it has fired three times in one batch.
  - **TWO STEM REPAIRS, and the corroboration is what made them safe.** Properties 02-23 and 03-29 both resolve to an ugly **35.104°** against option sets of 30/45/60/75 — because each has **one side label swapped**. Substituting `a/b` for `a/c` (and `a/c` for `a:b` in the mirror row) gives the 30-60-90 triangle, makes the keyed 30° correct, and is confirmed independently by **02-23's own parenthetical "(middle angle in AP)"** — true of 30, 60, 90 and false for the 35.1-84.9-60 triangle the printed data describes. Repairing the OPTION instead would have kept a corrupt stem and shipped an answer visibly foreign to its own option set. **The two rows being mirror images of each other is what shows the defect is systematic rather than a typo.**
  - **PROPERTIES OF TRIANGLE IS THE MOST DEFECTIVE CHAPTER THE EXAM HAS SEEN: 10 of 16 disputes had NO correct option**, plus one impossible triangle (b=6, c=7, B=60° forces sinC = 1.0104 > 1, and the cosine rule's discriminant is −3) and one ambiguous-SSA row keyed "No real solution" when **two** genuine triangles exist. Those last two are the **first exclusions of the exam** — no option repair rescues a question whose given data is self-contradictory, and repairing the stem would mean inventing a side. The signature elsewhere is a plausible stem, the wrong quantity computed, and four options none of which answers the question asked: `tan((A+B)/2) = cot(C/2)` carries **no side factor at all**, yet all four options attach one.
  - **PROBABILITY IS THE CLEANEST CHAPTER OF THE EXAM: 222 questions, ZERO wrong keys.** Its defect is entirely the unreduced-fraction twin (4/10 beside 2/5, 26/52 beside 1/2, 6/36 beside 1/6), with row 01-24 carrying two such pairs at once. Against that, **Definite Integration's file 01 runs 8 wrong keys in 30 rows (27%)** — pure answer-key-COLUMN corruption, since every one of those eight source solutions derives the correct value, the same signature as Binary Numbers' file 01 in Batch I.
  - **3D GEOMETRY'S GENERATOR SIGNATURE IS UNMISTAKABLE ONCE SEEN: it writes the square root of a PERFECT SQUARE as though it were a surd** — 6/√49 beside 6/7, 20/√25 beside 4, 12/√9 beside 4, 3/√27 beside 1/√3 — so row after row prints its own key a second time. The chapter's trap cuts both ways and every repair was tested mechanically: direction RATIOS and plane equations scaled by a non-zero constant are the same answer, whereas direction COSINES are not, since only one member of a proportional family satisfies l²+m²+n²=1.
  - **THE AUDITS EARNED THEIR PLACE AFTER COMMIT, WHICH IS WHERE THEY RUN.** `audit:keys` flagged 14. Three are probe false positives (it reads `P(A)` as "option A"). **Two were caused by my own key flips** — the source solution still named the pre-flip letter. The other **nine are a stale-option-letter defect running through the source**: solutions naming letters that do not match the printed order (y = x³+5 is option D and the solution calls it C; (3,4,5) is B and the solution calls it A; (2,4,6)&(1,2,3) is D and the solution says B). Every key was blind-confirmed correct — only the labels were wrong. All 11 rewritten to state the mathematical conclusion and **name no letter at all**, which removes the contradiction permanently rather than relocating it. 14 → 3.
  - **SOURCE SOLUTIONS THAT REFUTE THEMSELVES, now a documented genre.** DiffEq 02-6 derives option D across five numbered steps and writes "Option A is correct"; 02-17 reaches the right equation at Step 5 and keys a different one; 03-20 separates the variables correctly then declares at Step 4 that "the standard simpler separation" is dy/(1+y²) = cos x dx, plain algebra error and the origin of its wrong key. DefInt 01-17 computes 0 correctly, then writes "Wait, recalculate… Hmm, 0 gives B… So answer is B not A" — **talking itself into an answer its own arithmetic refutes** (option B is 4; the keyed A is 0). Conics 01-7 computes Δ = 0 and then calls the conic an ellipse anyway; it is genuinely degenerate, the single point (1, ½), and since no option offers "a point" the key is retained on the discriminant classification with the degeneracy stated plainly in the rewritten solution rather than hidden.
  - **AOD 03-11 and 03-12 ask about the SAME function and the source's two answers are MUTUALLY IMPOSSIBLE** — together they assert local maxima at x ≈ −0.59 and x = 1 with only one minimum between them, and a differentiable function cannot have two consecutive maxima with no minimum separating them. Both keys wrong. **Consistency between sibling questions is a check no single-row probe performs.**
  - **The Batch I shuffle bug did NOT recur** — verified by diffing every flagged row against every shuffle plan, none present, so the widened `isShuffleEligible` guard is holding. Six of eight chapters rebalanced to exactly 25% per letter; **Differential Equations (A 29% / D 22%) and Applications of Derivatives (A 32% / C 32% / D 13%) ship off-target deliberately**, because most of their rows name an option letter in the solution and the planner correctly refuses to force a move rather than have us rewrite dozens of correct solutions. Recorded rather than quietly skipped, as with Binary Numbers in Batch I.

- **2026-08-14 — Cadetprep Worksheets Batch I: Matrices and Determinants (290) + Inverse Trigonometry (173) + Inequalities (212) + Binary Numbers (85) = 760 q PUBLIC; the exam reaches 21 chapters / 6,121 q, the bank 44,999.** Twenty blind packets fanned out at once, then a second independent blind pass over all 84 disagreements. **Note this exam is otherwise UNDOCUMENTED in this file** — "Worksheets - 11th+12th" (`9fabd0f7-…`, slug `worksheets-11-12`, `practiceOnly`) is the LWS Cadetprep NDA Maths worksheet course, ingested Concept-Practice-only since 2026-08-05 and tracked in [[worksheets-ingestion]] + `scripts/worksheets/README.md`; at 6,121 q it is the second-largest exam in the bank and the header's exam list should name it.
  - **THE FINDING, and it is a trap this project has now hit twice: two independent blind passes agreeing on a letter is NOT sufficient evidence for a key flip.** Fifteen of the 34 Inequalities "disagreements" were illusory — `4) Modulus-Inequality.xls` duplicates its keyed answer **verbatim into option A** (04-44 carries it three times), so both passes derived the correct SET and simply named the first letter holding it. Acting on the crosstab at face value would have shipped 13 wrong flips on rows whose keys were right. The converse also fired: `05-27` in Inverse Trig was auto-flagged as a possible twin and is a genuine flip, because 1/(1+x²) and 1/(x²+2x+2) differ. **The rule is now step 4b of the runbook with its own `FLIP?TWIN` bucket: open the row and compare the two option TEXTS before touching a key.**
  - **The dual-blind protocol was promoted from scratch to pipeline** — `recheck.ts` (blind packet of disputed rows only: no key, no pass-1 verdict) + `crosstab.ts`, and pass-2 verdicts are now COMMITTED as `data/<id>.recheck.json` because they are the evidence behind every retained key. Pass 2 must return a `value` field stating what it computed in plain terms, so the crosstab compares two derivations of the same QUANTITY rather than two letter guesses.
  - **A real pipeline bug, silent and answer-affecting.** `isShuffleEligible`'s letter-reference guard required the letter to follow "option" IMMEDIATELY, so "Hence, the correct option is C" slipped through; five Binary rows were rebalanced and the key moved out from under text that still named the old letter. `audit:keys` caught it as `SOLN_C!=KEY_A` — the probe earning its keep. Broadened to a bounded same-sentence gap after option/answer/choice/correct, with the **letter half deliberately case-SENSITIVE**: matching the English article "a" would make most of the corpus ineligible and silently kill the rebalance instead of corrupting it. Four TDD cases. Critically, the fix was then verified NOT to change the already-committed shuffle plans of the three chapters already in the DB (byte-identical diff) — a changed plan would have desynced the committed files from the live rows.
  - **Per-chapter character, and the source keeps finding new ways to be wrong.** M&D ran 9.0% disagreement, its signature being `6)Determinant-Calculation.xls` where the AI solution derives the correct value and then overrides itself ("Determinant = −2 − 13 = −15. Recalculating properly gives 11"), leaving **five of six rows with no correct option at all**. Binary's 9 flips are ALL in one file (9 of 29 rows, 31%) and are pure key-column corruption on elementary definitions — byte = 8 bits, nibble = 4 bits, LSB = rightmost — where **the source's own solutions are correct and six of them name the right letter in words**, making those the most strongly evidenced flips of the batch. Inequalities 10 real flips, Inverse Trig only 4 (2.3%) but 8 no-correct-option rows. Batch: 34 key flips, ~45 option/stem repairs, 0 exclusions.
  - **Binary ships at 20/26/32/22 rather than 25% per letter, deliberately.** Almost every one of its solutions names an option letter, so almost no row is shuffle-eligible and the planner correctly refuses to force a move; rebalancing would mean rewriting 45 solutions that are already correct. Correctness over cosmetics, recorded rather than quietly skipped. The other three chapters all hit exactly 25%.
  - **Two of my own errors, both caught by the standing audits rather than by re-reading my work:** a repair set Inequalities 04-24's option A to a value option B already carried (a fresh duplicate), and four Binary rows had correct keys and correct reasoning but a closing sentence naming the wrong letter ("16, which is option C" — 16 is option D). Fixing the latter required a direct DB patch, because **`content_hash` excludes `solution`, so a solution-only override never reaches the database through commit's upsert**; anything touching options, stem or key needs DELETE + re-commit instead.

- **2026-08-13 — Bare `/browse` stopped running a 2.1 s query and became a cached starting panel; the five `/guide/nda-maths` sub-pages became genuinely prerendered. Merges `50012a1` + `d5ddf12`.** Opened by a Vercel usage chart that had roughly doubled since Jul 28 — and most of the session's value was in retiring three of my own wrong explanations for it.
  - **THE METRIC DECIDES THE DIAGNOSIS, and it took four wrong turns to learn that.** The chart is **Fluid Active CPU**, not wall-clock duration. A function `await`ing Postgres burns no CPU, so *every* SQL-side remedy — the 2.1 s query, the 378 daily statement timeouts, the retry loop — moves that line by approximately nothing. Explanations offered and killed, in order: **row growth** (correlation only — `db_health` starts 2026-08-07 and `pg_stat_statements` was reset 2026-08-09, so nothing can show per-call latency across the Jul 28 step; untestable, retracted); **DB latency** (arithmetically impossible — 1,317 successes × ~1.5 s + 352 timeouts × 3 s ≈ 51 min/day against a 17 min/day chart, a component cannot exceed its total); **a 3× retry amplifier** (refuted by the gap distribution between cancellations — a 3-attempt retry lands cancels ~3.25 s and ~3.75 s apart and **that band is empty**, 133 of 149 gaps are >15 s). What actually explains it: **traffic roughly doubled** (Vercel Analytics: visitors 2,969, page views 12,368, both ~+110%) and `/browse` is the most-visited page *and* uncacheable by construction. **[[shell-component-decaches-site]] already said "Active CPU counts CPU, not I/O wait" in plain words — the lesson existed and was not consulted.**
  - **The per-route panel was the thing worth asking for, and it should have been asked for first.** `/browse` = **964 invocations / 2m Active CPU (~41% of ~291 s per 12h)**, at 124 ms per invocation — *below* the ~150 ms fleet average. So `/browse` is expensive by VOLUME, not by weight, and there is nothing to shave inside the render; the only lever is serving fewer of them. That killed the "stop shipping `solution` in the list payload" idea as a CPU fix (it remains an egress idea). Second bucket: `/notes` + `/guide` + `/questions` at ~34% on only ~200 invocations (~500 ms each) — and a build proved those pages ARE correctly prerendered (`●`, `.html` on disk), so that third of the chart is not a config bug and has no cheap fix.
  - **What the bare page now renders, and why it is NOT a directory.** Measured: **48% of `/browse` renders carry no filters** (683 of 1,415 over 24h), 31% exam-only, 11% chapter/subtopic. The unfiltered branch skips the question query entirely and renders exam start-pills + the 8 densest chapters, from three already-cached reads (`getCachedExamCatalog`, `getExamIdMap`, `listChapterLandings` — all already paid for by `/`, the header and `/questions`). **Every link applies a filter IN PLACE.** The first design sketched exam cards + links to the 317 chapter pages, which is precisely the union of `/` and `/questions` — a third copy of a job two shipped pages already own. `/` is the front door, `/questions` the directory, `/browse` the tool.
  - **The old default was "whatever we ingested last."** Ordering is `created_at DESC`, so on 2026-08-13 the front door of a bank holding 10,614 JEE and 8,259 NDA questions opened on **25 Maharashtra HSC Binomial-Distribution questions**. That is a product argument, not a performance one, and it was labelled as such: all three candidate designs saved identical cost, so choosing between them was taste, not data — `/browse`'s own bounce rate and click-through (Vercel Analytics → Routes) remain unmeasured.
  - **A COUNT SHOWN NEXT TO A LINK MUST BE THE COUNT ITS DESTINATION SHOWS — got this wrong twice.** First the pills used `ExamCatalogItem.totalPublicQuestions` (pyq **+** practice, right for the homepage whose cards go to guides/notes): NDA would advertise **8,259** against a destination of 4,860. Then they summed `listChapterLandings`, which gets the *kind* right but silently drops every chapter under `MIN_QUESTIONS_FOR_LANDING = 15` — rendered **4,699**. I had checked ONE exclusion (zero null `chapter_id`) and declared the sum exact; a second exclusion existed. Fixed by asking directly: `getDefaultViewCountsByExam` (new, cached, one head count per exam, applying the same practice-only rule `/browse` itself uses). **The error was cured by rendering both numbers and diffing them, not by better reasoning about which rows are excluded where.**
  - **`isBareBrowse` deliberately does NOT reuse the page's `countActiveFilters`** — that helper ignores `extraIds` and `fit`, both of which narrow the result set, so `?fit=answerable` would have shown a whole-bank panel to someone who asked for a subset. It compares the whole filter set against `EMPTY_FILTERS`, and a test walks every key of `Filters` so a new field cannot slip through. **Org members keep the live list**: their reads include own-org PRIVATE rows the PUBLIC-only cached catalog cannot see.
  - **The five `/guide/nda-maths` sub-pages** (compound-tricks · principles · strategy · traps · trends) each built the cookie-bound server client, making the route dynamic and silently voiding their declared `revalidate = 86400` — the third instance of this shape after `AppHeader` (2026-07-29) and `/` (2026-08-13). Every other subject already used the anon client; nda-maths was the sole outlier as the first guide built. **Nothing rendered changes for anyone**: the one nda-maths-specific loader is `loadPrincipleStats`, and **0 of the 488 principle-tagged questions are PRIVATE**. Note `principles/[slug]` still uses the server client yet builds `●` — unexplained, and left alone precisely because it is already static.
  - **Verified from artifacts, not reasoning.** Gate PASS (238 test files + 19 prod-contract); prerendered `.html` **625 → 630**, the five new files on disk; against a production build, **8 anonymous `/browse` loads moved the unfiltered query's call count 1,313 → 1,313**, and every pill matches its destination exactly (NDA 4,860 · MHT-CET 6,615 · JEE 10,493). `?fit=answerable` and `?page=2` correctly fall through to the live list. **One probe was invalid and had to be re-run**: the first `next start` failed to bind because the previous server had not died, so the smoke test read the PREVIOUS build — which is the only reason the 4,699 defect surfaced at all ([[probe-must-reach-the-code]], again).
  - **Deliberately NOT done, with reasons:** `get_pyq_years` still runs on this path (~1,300 calls/day, ~16 ms, ~21 s/day) — it is `security invoker`, and [[next-caching-traps]] already forbids caching those against the anon client; today `years_only_in_private = 0` makes it *look* safe, but that is a coincidence of the current corpus with 3,931 PRIVATE rows and continuous ingestion, not an invariant. Migration **0077 has no `schema_migrations` row** — but the file is `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS`, so replay is a no-op; this is hygiene, **not** the 0021 class (whose *file* was missing). `loadPrincipleStats` passes **488 ids to one `.in()`** (~18 kB URL; known failure at 833 ≈ 31 kB) and **swallows the error** (`if (error) return stats`), so a 400 would bake an empty stats block into a now-prerendered page for 24 hours — the swallow is the defect, not the id count.

- **2026-08-13 — A false alarm in the health tracker led to the two real costs left on this database: the homepage was uncached (38 min of DB time in 4 days) and `question_kind` was missing from every index (`/browse` 1,986 → 5.3 ms). Migrations 0076 + 0077.** Opened by a `spill-unattributed` warning; the warning itself was wrong, and chasing it was still worth it.
  - **THE WARNING WAS THE TRACKER MISREADING ITS OWN NOTES.** It reported 113 MB spilled with "only 0.9 MB" accounted for — while **177 MB sat in the same `queries` array it was reading**. Four of the six spilling queries were first-seen, and the coverage rule dropped every first-seen row. (0.9 MB is exactly the two surviving rows' deltas — confirmed arithmetically, not inferred.) **The fix could not be "include new rows":** an existing test rightly defends against a newly-COLLECTED query laundering months of lifetime history into one window, which is what 0070 caused when 100 queries appeared at once. The discriminator used instead is a **physical bound, not a heuristic — no single query can have spilled more IN THIS WINDOW than the whole database did.** Under the bound a first-seen figure is credible; over it, it is provably lifetime and still ignored. The defending test passes unchanged.
  - **A MONITOR SET AT THE SAME POINT AS THE AUTOMATIC REMEDIATION CANNOT CARRY INFORMATION.** The dead-row rule warned at 20%; autovacuum fires at `50 + 0.2 × live` = 9,415 rows on `questions`, and the rule fired at 9,365 — **the same point**. Its message even said "autovacuum usually clears this on its own", true by construction. It had never fired in eleven snapshots, across a healthy sawtooth of 7.7 → 18.3 → 7.2%. Raised to 35%, where firing means autovacuum is genuinely losing. Filed as rule 8 of [[monitor-threshold-calibration]].
  - **THE HOMEPAGE WAS MISSED BY THE 2026-07-29 DE-CACHING SWEEP.** `/` declares `revalidate = 86400`, but reads cookies (to redirect staff to `/dashboard`) BEFORE fetching — so the directive never took effect and its **12 per-exam head-counts ran on every anonymous request**: 3,921 calls at 585 ms = ~38 minutes of DB time in four days, for numbers that change only on ingest. The July sweep fixed `AppHeader`; **a fix to a shared component does not cover a page that reads cookies directly.** Now behind `unstable_cache` (anon client ⇒ cache-legal). Verified on a production build: **5 loads → 12 DB calls instead of 60**, counts matching the bank exactly, cache-miss and cache-hit renders identical, and the signed-in redirect confirmed by hand. **Only the DB half is cached** — registry order, flags, hrefs and the notes lookup stay outside, so a new exam appears on the next deploy rather than waiting out a 24-hour entry. The trap that shaped the payload: **`unstable_cache` serialises its result and a `Map` serialises to `{}`** — cache one and every card silently prints "0 questions" with no error; entry arrays are used instead, pinned by two tests.
  - **`/browse`: the sort was never the problem, and neither was the count.** After the 2026-08-05 two-phase fix the sort is a **28 kB top-N heapsort — free**. The remaining cost was that `questions_public_filters_idx` covers `(visibility, exam_id, subject_id, created_at)` but **not `question_kind`**, so an exam+kind query pulled 9,224 rows from the index and visited the heap for every one purely to test the kind, discarding 4,364. **The evidence was already in the database:** the identical shape filtered by `subtopic_id + question_kind` runs at **5.9 ms across 12,736 calls** because `questions_subtopic_kind_idx` covers both — same shape, ~350× apart, one variable. The measured post-fix 5.3 ms landed almost exactly on that prediction. **A natural experiment sitting in production beats any estimate.**
  - **INDEX FIRST, THEN VACUUM — the reverse of the order this session originally proposed.** The index alone took the kind-only shape 4,454 → 705 ms, not the hoped ~6 ms, because it had become an **Index Only Scan still doing 12,695 heap fetches**. That is the distinction: an **Index Scan + Filter is vacuum-proof** (the heap visit is the filter), while an **Index Only Scan + heap fetches is exactly what vacuum fixes**. The index converted one into the other, and only then did `VACUUM (ANALYZE)` pay: coverage **67.4% → 100%**, dead rows 17.5% → 0%, heap fetches **13,092 → 0**, 705 → 228 ms. Vacuuming first would have bought ~16% and looked like a dead end.
  - **BUILDING THE INDEX FOR REAL BEAT TRIALLING IT — the user's challenge, and they were right.** A trial must run inside a transaction to roll back, and **`CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY` cannot run inside a transaction** — so the "safe" trial forces the *write-blocking* build while the real thing blocks nothing. Same lock cost, worse lock, and only an `EXPLAIN` estimate instead of live traffic. Verified `indisvalid`/`indisready` afterwards: a failed concurrent build leaves an invalid index that is unused but must be dropped, not ignored.
  - **Cost, stated rather than buried:** the 17th index on the most heavily-written table, 3.2 MB (total 31 → 34 MB). The write-amplification trade was declined once before and is taken now only because the read side was measured. **The write cost itself is unmeasured** — the next ingest is what settles whether it stays. Also unresolved: 100% coverage will decay under ingestion (which is why 0076 tracks it), and the 80% threshold is set from a single reading, so it may prove too chatty. Both recorded in the code and in `SUGGESTIONS.md`. Incidentally: the **largest chapter is now 664 PUBLIC questions** (was 531 six days earlier) against the tracker's 800 warn and the PostgREST 1000 cap.

- **2026-08-13 — MH HSC Class-12 board PYQs shipped: 317 questions PUBLIC across ALL 15 Maths chapters, every sitting 2015-2025. New pipeline `scripts/mh-hsc-12-pyq/`. 22 source defects adjudicated against the printed pages — 18 ours, 4 the board's.**
  - **A SECOND pipeline into the SAME exam and the SAME chapters, separated by `question_kind`** — the `mh-ssc-10` + `mh-ssc-10-text` shape. `mh-hsc-12` is no longer `practiceOnly`: the flag tracks whether an exam HAS past-year questions, not which corpus is larger (the textbook side is still ~8× bigger). Deliberately NOT reusing `scripts/stateboard`'s `buildRecords`: it requires a `bucket` that drives `/board`'s `section_*` book-structure axis, and a board PYQ has no place in the book's structure — PYQ rows carry the conceptual axis (subtopic) only.
  - **THE SOURCE IS A COMPILATION, NOT THE PAPERS, and that is the load-bearing fact.** 15 born-digital .docx, one per chapter, each question carrying a `[Q. n, Month Year]` provenance tag. That buys a clean pandoc path (no vision, no OCR) and costs completeness — 38-45 of the 44 questions in a paper — so this is a chapterwise bank and **cannot back a `/mock` sitting**. It also means the compilation is an LWS working document whose editorial additions carry no more authority than its transcriptions: **all four of its own switching-circuit descriptions are WRONG** against the extracted PNGs (one calls a series pair parallel, one contradicts itself), so the real figures are attached and the descriptions dropped.
  - **NO ANSWER KEY EXISTS ANYWHERE** — a board question paper never ships one. So all 60 MCQ keys are DERIVED and every model answer AUTHORED. Each key was derived TWICE independently: by the authoring pass (which sees the chapter's existing textbook corpus) and by a BLIND pass seeing stem and options only. **46 agree, 0 disagreements.** `reconcile-keys.ts` is deliberately a work list that never picks a winner, and it distinguishes a real conflict from **stale blind input** — a blind derivation made before the row's stem was corrected was reasoning about a different question, which described 5 of the mismatches exactly.
  - **THE DEFECT CLASS THAT MATTERS: a stem the compilation copied wrong, where the question still READS as well-formed.** Options intact, LaTeX balanced, arithmetic self-consistent — no probe finds these. Every one surfaced because a pass SOLVED the question and the answer came out ugly, degenerate or absent, then was settled against the printed page. **The page reversed the hypothesis in BOTH directions**, which is why each was read rather than reasoned about: `def#7` was called ours on the good argument that a board would not misprint a limit while leaving the RHS right for the intended one — it is the board's; `prob#16` was called the board's on the good argument that `2x+1` and `x+2` are not confusable for an extractor — true of OCR, false for a human-typed compilation, so it is ours. The sharpest single case: **`(2x+1)/18` integrates to EXACTLY 1 over (−2,4) and so passes the obvious p.d.f. check, yet is negative on part of its support** and is therefore not a density at all.
  - **A no-correct-option MCQ is a real and expected outcome, not a failure.** Three occurred. Each was a hypothesis about WHOSE defect it was until the page settled it: two ours (one where the compilation's own mis-copy had DELETED the correct answer from its option list) and one the board's, preserved with an erratum. A blind pass correctly refused to pick a letter on a question whose options we had made byte-identical.
  - **TWO CHAPTER MIS-FILINGS, both CAUSED by a transcription defect rather than a judgement call.** `indef#2` has neither "Evaluate:" nor "dx" on the page — both were glued on from the sibling item directly above it, making a pure differentiation question look like an integral. `def-integration#3` has NO LIMITS printed; we held a fabricated definite integral. New `chapterOverride` carries the move: the compilation files a question by the section it was typed into, so a stem that acquired an integral sign from its neighbour acquired that neighbour's chapter too.
  - **GUARDS THAT EARNED THEIR KEEP, each after a real failure.** Every repair matches its adjudicated text EXACTLY and REFUSES otherwise — which caught a fix aimed at a row whose text is copied in from the dropped half of a duplicate pair during dedupe, i.e. at a string that does not exist at repair time. A fix whose `from` equals its `to` is now refused outright: one shipped as a silent no-op because it was built with `.replace()` against a needle a shell layer had mangled, and it passed every check while correcting nothing. A known MCQ whose options were lost is refused rather than shipped as free-response — a silent format downgrade invisible in every count. And `commit.ts` NAMES every row the exam-scoped `content_hash` absorbs into an existing textbook row, because in a PYQ ingest a silent `skipped=1` is indistinguishable from a question that was never on the paper.
  - **The extractor's own output was wrong in five ways that only showed once rows were live**, all found by `audit:text`: pandoc's default markdown emits "simple tables" (a dashed grid nothing here parses) for five probability-distribution tables — switching to GFM pipe tables then exposed that `collapseSpaces` joined every newline, and **a pipe table IS its line structure**; escaped `\<`/`\>` on every p.d.f. support interval; a flattened fill-in blank shipping as `\:`; a lone `\` before the board's internal-choice `OR`; and three lost superscripts shipping as literal `x2`. Changing the extractor invalidated every adjudicated fix and the guard refused them all — correctly — so they were migrated by applying the same artifact rules to both `from` and `to`.
  - **All 317 rows were deleted and re-committed rather than edited**, since `content_hash` covers the stem; the wipe asserts its own scope first because the textbook corpus shares these chapters. Verified per chapter by reading back FROM the database: 15/15 clean, `audit:text` clean on all five classes, `board:lint` green, 5 circuit figures attached. See [[mh-hsc-12-pyq-ingestion]].

- **2026-08-11 — Local database backups shipped (`npm run db:backup`, pure core + 20 TDD cases, Windows task every Monday 11:00) — the project had NO backup mechanism of any kind.**
  - **WHY IT WAS NEEDED, stated as an audit rather than a feeling.** Much of the bank is reproducible from the repo — 1,433 tracked data JSONs, 88 JEE paper files — but **zero `.xlsx` are tracked**, so the NDA + MHT-CET corpora (~14,000 q, the two oldest subjects) have no source of record here at all; the 13-subject taxonomy cleanup was done as **inline SQL in chat** ("no scripts", per the workflow note above), so 513 chapters + 4,941 subtopics exist only as rows; and the ~235 wrong-key flips were applied to rows, some mirrored back to source and some not (the `scripts/stateboard` MCQ mirror hole in the backfill ledger is a live instance). On top of that ~15,500 operational rows — `paper_questions` 3,431 · `attempt_answers` 7,756 · `user_activity` 2,090 · `question_reviews` 1,312 · `student_profiles` 98 — have **no source anywhere**.
  - **THE THREAT MODEL IS OUR OWN SCRIPTS, NOT SUPABASE, and that decided the design.** This log is a list of near-misses from bulk writes: the unscoped NCERT prune that would have deleted the whole Physics spine (2026-08-03), `derive-board-status` that would have destroyed 4,315 hand-authored rows without its `--force` guard (2026-08-05), `scan-flip` publishing two withheld Maths rows (2026-08-01), the review-dump that silently destroyed filled-in verdicts (2026-08-11). Every one was caught by a guard or by luck. So the highest-value invocation is **by hand before a risky script**, and the weekly cron is the safety net — not the other way round.
  - **TWO ROUTES ELIMINATED, both by testing rather than reasoning.** (1) **The Supabase CLI shells out to DOCKER** — `supabase db dump` returns `LegacyDockerRunError`, and Docker Desktop (multi-GB + a background VM, on a drive with 15 GB free) is the wrong price for a weekly backup. (2) **A hand-rolled TypeScript export was the tempting zero-install option and is the wrong tool**: reading tables through PostgREST inherits the 1000-row cap that has bitten this codebase five times — **silently**, producing a backup holding 2% of the bank that looks fine — and it **cannot reach the `auth` schema at all**, so all 102 accounts would be absent and every row keyed to them would restore orphaned. pg_dump reads from one snapshot, fails loudly, and covers `auth`. Verified: `auth.users` is in the dump.
  - **The CLI dry-run was still worth running, for two things it printed.** Supabase's **own pg_dump flag set** (the authoritative list of schemas their tooling omits, now the basis of `EXCLUDED_SCHEMAS` — kept as a DENY list so a new custom schema is backed up rather than silently dropped, the `needsBuild` polarity argument); and the fact that the CLI **provisions a temporary `cli_login_postgres` role**, so it needs no database password. **Caveat worth knowing: `--dry-run` prints that role's live password to the console in plain text** — never paste its output anywhere.
  - **The direct DB host is IPv6-ONLY** (`db.<ref>.supabase.co` publishes AAAA and **no A record**). It works on the current network and will fail with a confusing connection error on any network without IPv6; the session pooler is the IPv4 fallback, with a different username format. Checked before writing a line of the script, which is the only reason it wasn't discovered at 11:00 on a Monday.
  - **A REAL BUG IN MY OWN RETENTION CODE, caught in self-review before it shipped.** `--keep=abc` parses to `NaN`; `Math.max(1, NaN)` is `NaN`; `slice(0, NaN)` is `[]` — so the naive version marked **every backup for deletion** on a typo, in the one function whose entire job is deciding what to delete. Now every degenerate limit (NaN, Infinity, 0, negative, fractional) resolves towards keeping MORE, and the CLI rejects a garbage `--keep` loudly. Two sibling safety properties are spec'd: it never returns a file whose name it cannot parse (so a stray in `backups/` is not its business), and retention is by RUN so a dump and its manifest can never be separated. See [[destructive-default-fails-safe]].
  - **VERIFIED, not asserted.** 24 MB in 16 s; the archive read back through `pg_restore` as **85.3 MB of SQL, 71 COPY blocks**, with `questions` **45,351** and `auth.users` **102** matching the live counts exactly. **The size reconciles completely** — 169 MB on disk − 58 MB of indexes (derived data, rebuilt on restore, correctly never dumped) − 11 MB page/TOAST overhead = **89 MB heap → 85.3 MB as SQL → 24 MB at compression level 9 (3.6×)**. A truncated dump would show as wrong row counts, not as a small file.
  - **REGISTERING A SCHEDULED TASK IS NOT PROOF IT RUNS.** Fired it through the scheduler: `LastTaskResult 0`, a third dump written, `backups/last-run.log` appended by `weekly.cmd` (a scheduled run's console output otherwise goes nowhere). `-StartWhenAvailable` is the load-bearing flag — without it a sleeping laptop skips the week silently.
  - **My own failure-path probe never reached the failure path.** Running with a deliberately wrong password dumped production successfully, because every script here loads `.env.local` with `override: true` and the file beats the environment. Recorded in the runbook; the rejection path is therefore proven **by construction** (`fail()` exits before the pruning block) and by unit test, **not empirically** — stated as a gap rather than papered over. See [[probe-must-reach-the-code]].
  - **Scope deliberately NOT taken:** encryption (a mitigation for uploading somewhere shared — these stay local), an off-machine copy (the user's call; `C:` already holds the only copies of the source PDFs and Excel uploads, so that exposure predates this), and a restore rehearsal into the test project (it would copy production PII into a second Supabase account and clobber the test fixtures). Runbook: `scripts/backup/README.md`. See [[local-database-backup]].
- **2026-08-11 — BLIND re-derivation of two LWS papers (180 q): 176 agree · 4 disagree · 3 wrong keys corrected — and the one that mattered was invisible to a read-through.** The user asked for the solutions of the 120-q NDA Maths Full Mock + the 60-q Properties-of-Triangle/Inverse-Trig/Trig-Equations paper to be reviewed. Done twice: first a read-through (`solution_audit`), then a genuinely blind pass via agents.
  - **THE READ-THROUGH found 2 wrong keys and 1 broken solution**, each with a distinct tell. **Q941** ("variance of 2,4,6,8") was keyed 8 while **its own stored solution derives 5** — a key/solution contradiction. **Q2017** (shortest distance between two lines) was keyed (2,3] while the true value is **1.7263 ∈ [1,2)**, and the tell was that the solution *never computed anything* — it said "the printed solution evaluates ≈ 2.7". **A solution that cites an authority instead of deriving is unverified by definition.** **Q51** (relation "x−y zero or irrational") had the right answer and a solution that was visible failed scratch work: four attempted counterexamples, every one of which SATISFIES transitivity, then "the standard result is…". Rewritten with one that works (√2, 0, √2−1: two irrational steps whose difference is 1).
  - **THE BLIND PASS found a third — and this is the whole argument for keeping the two methods apart.** **Q1217**: option B lists **only VALID** solutions of `tan((π/2)sin θ) = cot((π/2)cos θ)`, so nothing about it reads as wrong.
